W: By practicing and doing your PAZ homework you will be able to HOME EDUCATION FORUM MORE LOGOUT identify the direction of price with a much higher success rate. Once Posts: 359 Thank you received: you are able to identify the direction you can then focus on the best 571 spot to enter. My current understanding of PAZs: - Essentially, from my understanding, PAZ is simply the space between FLs. - To use this for our trading, we should just remember that when price enters a new PAZ from one side, price would then want to continue to go to the other side of the new PAZ. - To enter a new PAZ, price need to engulf the previous PAZ's FL. In RTM, we are taught that after price engulfs into a new PAZ without hitting the other side of the new PAZ, we can enter on the retrace to the FL that caused the engulf (many call this the DP/Decision Point, which, from what I understand, is also a FL). - Since PAZs are defined as the space between FLs, then naturally there would be PAZ between PCP MPL and base of CP MPL, between two ends of a pole (LQ gap or wide range bars), and between the top and bottom of flags. The best trades should be taken at the edges of these PAZs, i.e. not inside a PAZ. - With the above logic, then we should be able to classify the regions on the chart as either a FL or a PAZ. - PAZs, like price movement, are fractal in nature as well. What looks like a single PAZ in one timeframe can have many FLs and smaller PAZs inside of it. The choice on how we should define our PAZs is ours to make according to which timeframe/scale we want to trade. - This part is if I didn't misunderstood bro Staurum and bro Enkim: Something that I learned from bro Staurum is that we should always look at the higher scale picture for any possible sign of FO that could happen to the lower scale price movement. Here is the post that hinted on this issue. The clue is to look at the HTF picture. Always make sure that the price movement in the HTF has already cleared the MPL of that HTF FL before trying to trade LTF FLs because if the HTF MPL hasn't been hit, price could fake past all of the LTF FLs and go for the HTF MPL, even if there was a valid engulf to the next PAZ edge. Bro Enkim wrote a note ( here ) that I understood only a few days ago. Thank you bro Staurum and bro Enkim - If price directly hits the other edge of a PAZ, then all trades are off until there is another engulf into another PAZ or if there is a LTF PA telling us to stay within the PAZ (this case, I believe is what we call flagging). Apologies for the lack of charts, but I think the concept of "spaces between FLs" should be a clear picture if FLs has been properly understood. As always, I appreciate any comments, hints, and corrections on this matter. With the usual self-help ramble done, hopefully, with these few posts, I can finally move on to the other processes other than technical aspect of trading, i.e. money management and, most importantly, psychology. I will start with the Engulf and Fakeout. From the markepedia: Engulf: P: "There is no Price Action without an Engulf" and it is what defines our trading. It is our leading "indicator" regarding price intentions. W: Forget about candle engulfs/engulfing patterns. What we look for is Flag Limit Engulfs. Through homework and experience you will HOME EDUCATION FORUM MORE LOGOUT learn which FL engulfs are real PA. Once a FL is engulfed price will want to go to the next, but we always have to be mindful of how much it can retrace. My current understanding of Engulfs: - Real engulfs or PAs are defined as the consumption of all orders inside FLs, i.e. Engulfs are not simply breaks of local SR, highs and lows, FTRs which are not FLs, or random supply demand zones. I always try to keep this one simple: If I can't find the FL that I think a price movement engulfs, just find the next clear FLs to see the engulf from and scratch the previous mentioned engulf interpretation. - I always try to remember to draw FLs properly first before assessing engulfs from them. The markepedia gave us a hint on this: Through homework and experience you will learn which FL engulfs are real PA. What looks like a PA/engulf, could be a reaction off the real FL or true supply/demand which is obscured inside a large candle in the tick chart. Please see the following example for a real chart example: - The only useful engulf is the one that hasn't reached its target. We should only look for these types of engulfs. Engulfs that has reached its target is non-tradeable - About this part in the markepedia: Once a FL is engulfed price will want to go to the next, but we always have to be mindful of how much it can retrace, through screen time, I concluded that this has to do with the HTF FL that we want to trade from. The important question to ask about an engulf is: Did the engulf came from the last clean sets of orders, i.e. from the MPL? I would be very careful of trying to trade from a FL if previous price movement didn't clear the HTF FL MPL first beforehand, since a DM of the HTF price action could form after an engulf of a LTF FL if the ITs decide to clear all the orders in the HTF FL. This was my first question in this post and the answer to that question is summed up in the previous sentences. In short: Always be mindful of the HTF picture, particularly their MPLs - A personal recipe of master Salivan's engulf, which can be read from the FOF Article states that the ITs can show their intention by engulfing LTF FL, while at the same time, not engulfing the HTF FL. Personally, for a noob HOME EDUCATION FORUM MORE LOGOUT trader such as myself, I believe it is best to leave this advanced master class concept for when we understand all other RTM puzzle pieces. I have not proven this concept myself, but I believe it is safer to wait for whole FL engulfs because there might be clean orders inside an even lower chart than the 1 minute chart, e.g. 10-second or tick charts, above or below the given 1-minute FL. - An important article from master Salivan is the Engulf/Retrace Article . The conclusion that, I think, we should understand from this article is that: an engulf which didn't reach its' target, would surely have left a set or more clean orders from the FL it came from. It is the retrace to this price level with clean orders (FOF) that we should trade from. - Also from the engulf/retrace article, the depth of retrace after an engulf depends on whether the target is an important FL or not (i.e. FTR). Important FLs, from my studies, would have FOs in the form of QM levels that hasn't been touched. Master Mertch's charts show what these FOs look like. If the target is a FL with QM levels that are still clean, then a deeper retrace should be expected as these are ITs' price targets and they would want to get their last orders from the other side of the market to be closed in the FL with clean orders mentioned previously. - Master Staurum created a helpful, three part post on FLs, Engulfs, and Targets with Master Salivan's answer in his journal that is very beneficial for everyone here. - Also important from Master Harry: If you want to find good bouncy places you have to follow the order flow and see where it failed, where there are people (or robots) trapped. - To sum up the wordy, mind-dumping words above: The useful engulf is the one that hasn't reached its target, which signals us to trade in the direction of the engulf from the source FL of the engulf, i.e. from places where people/robots are trapped, while keeping in mind the HTF picture for possible DM/FO scenario As usual, this is what I have learned and not necessarily the truth. The charts, and not my or anyone's words, would provide the ultimate truth. Also, I would extremely appreciate any corrections in any of my flaws of thinking in any of the above points as I am still studying. Hopefully nothing is way off the mark Thank you all in advance! Next up would be the Fakeout and how to distinguish FO from engulf. Attachments: HOME EDUCATION FORUM MORE LOGOUT The following user(s) said Thank You: Ifmyante, Kavya, Rinehart, Astrokillah Crede quod habes, et habes QUICK REPLY REPLY QUOTE THANK YOU About Origins, Targets, Engulf, Fakeout, Ignored Levels, QMs, and DMs - Part 2 28 Nov 2017 07:57 #234 Frosty Next up is the FO and what distinguishes them from engulfs Markepedia doesn't have an entry on this, but I believe it is important to understand them and differentiate them from engulfs. Offline Associate Trader Posts: 359 Thank you received: 571 My current understanding of Fakeouts, Origins/Targets, Ignored Levels, QM, and DM: - To distinguish between FO and engulf, we should look to the FL they are reacting to. While an engulf consumes the FL they are reacting to, an FO stays within the confines of the FL. Please see this modified diagram from IF for my understanding of FOs: HOME EDUCATION FORUM MORE LOGOUT - A great explanation from Master Mertch's journal can be seen here - FO's purpose is to create liquidity. They serve as targets and origins for ITs. These are the "tricks being played" part of the price action puzzle. - A DM should be expected if FO level doesn't quite touch the MPL of the FL price is reacting to. A DM is essentially, Imho, a FO of a FO level. - Targets and origins naturally has a double role. They both are one of the same thing. A long origin/source's target is a short origin/source and a short origin/source's target is a long origin/source. These are essentially LS kink / QM levels inside FLs. - We should check LTF for each QM levels inside the FL we are interested in if we are entering our positions as they may be spiked on the way away from the FL. Do not trade the second and subsequent touches to a QM level if we do not have engulf away from the particular QM level. 1st time back has the most chance of a bounce. - Quote: "Look for tricks being played on approach into places where tricks were played". My understanding of this quote: "Look for tricks being played on approach (BSZ, CP, CPLQ) into places where tricks were played (QM levels or LS kinks inside FLs). All should be done with proper PA/engulf in the right direction. - As for Ignored levels, after many hours of studies, I would stick to just iQM levels that was inside a FL, like in my homework above. My current understanding is that the FO level should be inside the FL to validate the iQM, if the QM level doesn't get respected on the way back. iQM levels that wasn't created inside a FL, I would personally pass. iQM levels would create the next swap FL. Ignored CPs and SRs are left out at the moment. I want to master a few things first at a time. - For QM and DM understanding, at this point in my education, I understand it all comes down to finding pockets of trapped traders and robots, i.e. clean sets of orders. These can be seen through a LS level that hasn't been touched on the way away and, preferably, create some sort of BSZ or CP on the way to the true supply or demand. If we were to zoom in HOME EDUCATION FORUM MORE LOGOUT on LTF, at the tip of a DM, we would likely see a miniature QM formation. DM and QM is a byproduct of the IT manipulation, imho. If MPL was taken by the QM's HH/LL, then QM level or LS kink will work. If MPL didn't get taken by the QM's HH/LL, then expect a DM to form. - My understanding on the details of DM: here - My understanding on the details of QM: here - Ultimately, in the simplest form, as master IF said in one of his videos, we are trading from where the retail stop losses are From all of these technical summaries, I would combine all of these knowledge together to move on to "reading a session" exercises. Associate Trader Previously had a demo account but I want to reset with a clean slate. This time I believe that I am more equipped in the psychological and technical Posts: 359 department Thank you received: Usual ramble about life: 571 The main breakthrough in the psychological side I had is the part where I should forgive myself for making mistakes. This realization removes most of the anxiety in trading. I am now comfortable of making mistakes without negative self-talk happening. I can't say the anxiety is all removed, though, as I am still a human and endless character development would be required. I can now differentiate better between fear and anxiety. HOME EDUCATION FORUM MORE LOGOUT What I have learned: Fear is a basic instinct. It keeps us alive and actions are usually immediate. For example, most people in close proximity to dangerous insects or animals would flee instantly. Anxiety, on the other hand, is self-limiting and keeps us immobile. Anxiety paralyzes the mind and hence, the body. This creates invisible boundaries on our capabilities and clouds our judgement. Most of our decisions that comes from anxiety would not be the best ones because we are thinking from an unresourceful state of mind. Anxiety, I believe, comes from irrational worry from going outside our own personal comfort zones. The solution to anxiety is, in my opinion, acceptance of self and forgiveness of self in situations where mistakes were made. Look at mistakes objectively, then learn from them without emotionally charging the event. Most of the time, when we look back on our past experiences, we are blowing the worry out of proportion and when we actually do what we are supposed to do, nothing as bad or catastrophic as the scenario in our minds occurs. Intentional immediate action is also a solution that works for me. I find that the longer I wait and think to do something that I worry about, more negativity comes into the mind. Probably because this topic seems like a common sense for everybody, but I would Associate Trader like to hear your opinion on this as well since it doesn't seem like a common sense to me. I apologize in advance if this topic is too basic to post as I want to understand all aspects of trading, not just the technical side of things. Posts: 359 To start, I have understood this one thing that everyone should do: we Thank you received: should never risk everything on a single trade. I understand that we should have many bullets, i.e. capital, to spread the risk for all of our 571 trades, unless we are the IT themselves, which would know where price is going to be next. Throughout my studies of the markets, there are many money management systems/methods that I have learned, but three in particular stood out. But they each have their own weaknesses as well. These three methods are: 1. Fixed dollar risk 2. Fixed percentage risk 3. Fixed lot size with a defined number of pips for stop loss Fixed Dollar Risk Method If we are using this method, we would set from the beginning a maximum fixed amount of dollars for each trade and we would adjust our lot size according to the fixed dollar amount that we have set at the beginning. For example, if we have a $10.000 account, we could set that we want to risk $100 maximum on each trade. Given that we would be getting a margin call from our broker when our account equity reaches $2.000, we would need to lose 80 times in a row before getting one. The important part here is that the dollar amount doesn't change for each successive trade. To calculate the lot size, we would use the formula below: Lot size= ($ Amount Risk Maximum)/(Lot pip x Amount of pip risk) For example, for a 100 pip risk on EUR/USD where the lot pip is 10 and $100 maximum risk, we would obtain 0.1 lots as our lot size. By using this lot size, we would ensure that the $100 maximum risk for the trade is HOME fulfilled. FORUM MORE LOGOUT EDUCATION The strength of this fixed dollar risk method is we would know in advance the dollar risk we are risking for each trade, considering there are no gaps or slippage. In the above example, we would know that if we lost 80 consecutive trades, then we would be down to $2.000. If this seems like a small or large dollar amount than expected, we could easily adjust the maximum dollar amount that we would be risking. Increasing the maximum dollar risk would leave us with smaller number of consecutive losses buffer until margin call, and the opposite is also logical. The weakness for this method is we would have fewer consecutive loss buffers than using the fixed percentage risk, which would have more consecutive losses buffer since the top part of the equation is always changing depending on the account balance. Fixed Percentage Risk Method If we are using this method, we would set a fixed percentage of the account balance that we would risk for each trade. The lot size would also naturally adjust to the dollars being risked. To calculate the lot size using this method, we would use the formula below: Lot size= (Percentage risk in decimal x $ Account balance)/(Lot pip x Amount of pip risk) For example, for a 100 pip risk on EUR/USD where the lot pip is 10 and 1% maximum risk on a $10.000 account, we would also obtain 0.1 lots as our lot size. By using this lot size, we would ensure that the 1% maximum risk for the trade is fulfilled. If we took a loss on the previous trade, then our account balance would be $9.900. The next trade we take would have $99 as the top part of the equation. Taking the same trade with the same risk parameter as above would obtain 0.09 lots (rounded down). The strength of this method is that we would automatically lower our risk the more losses we get. We would have a larger number of consecutive losses buffer. Also the size of our wins get larger the more wins we get. The weakness of this method comes when we have multiple positions across many different markets at the same time. It would be a hassle to re- calculate the lot sizes for all other pairs (assuming they are still pending orders) each time a loss on one trade occurs. The orders that are still open would have a relatively "higher" risk since the account balance would have changed by the time the loss occurred. So while this method has its big strength, it seems impractical from the strategy execution point of view. HOME EDUCATION FORUM MORE LOGOUT Another weakness also comes from the fact that we are using smaller lot sizes each time losses occur. The size of our wins would decrease significantly if we are relatively deep in drawdown (We shouldn't worry about this though if we understand RTM materials properly ) Fixed Lot Size with a Defined Number of Pips for Stop Loss Method If we are using this method, we would set a definite number of lots and a defined number of pips for stop loss. For example we could set to trade 0.1 lots and define the stop loss at 10 pips from our entry. The potential trades that don't fit into the 10 pip stop loss parameter is automatically discarded. The strength of this method is we wouldn't need to calculate the lot sizes and the stop loss pips each time we would want to enter a trade. If a potential trade fits the criteria, we enter the trade. If not, we pass. Decision making would be efficient if we are using this method. The weakness of this method comes from the changing value of the lot pip for non-base pairs. For example, the lot pip for AUD/USD would not be exactly 10 if we are using a CAD account, while AUD/CAD would be. Essentially, this would vary our risk from time to time, depending on the value of the lot pip. What I Personally Use I personally use the fixed percentage method for risk management, but I do not strictly adhere to the percentage risk parameter. What I mean by this is at any given time, whenever I want to open a position, I calculate the lot size depending on the present account balance (not equity). If a loss or a win occurs, I don't cancel the other pending orders and re- enter the positions according to the new risk percentage parameters. The percentage risk would not be fixed, but in a relatively safe range around the base percentage risk, which, personally, is 0.5% of account balance per trade. This simplifies my lot size calculations a lot. Correlation Considerations As an additional risk management method, I also use correlation considerations. I would limit my exposure to any given currency to a maximum of 3 at any given time to avoid overexposure, and hence imho, increasing the same type of currency risk. I would also limit the number of correlated markets to a maximum to 2 at any given time. Examples of correlated markets are, to my knowledge: Gold and silver, AUD/USD and NZD/USD, USD/CAD and CAD/JPY, EUR/USD and USD/CHF, one stock index with another stock index, and many more that I haven't listed here. The concept behind this is also not to increase the same type of currency risks or cancel trades out due to strong HOME EDUCATION FORUM MORE LOGOUT negative correlations. I don't have experience with this concept, though, and would appreciate any comments or guidance regarding this topic. I could be way off here as this is basically what I think I should do from information around the web and previous trading courses. Hoping to hear your opinions on this matter I apologize for the lengthy post The following user(s) said Thank You: HCDE Crede quod habes, et habes QUICK REPLY REPLY QUOTE THANK YOU Frosty's Igloo 06 Dec 2017 10:01 #246 zee Hi Frosty, Offline I find it best to combine the fixed dollar amount method with the fixed percent method. Easiest way to show it is with an example: Trader A 10 000$ account, risking 1% per trade. That would mean that as long as the account balance is between 10 000 Posts: 166 and 10 999, you risk 100$ per trade. Between 11 000 and 11 999 you risk Thank you received: 110$, so on... I think this gives you the best of both methods. If you have a loss or two, 296 but you are still in your range you don't need to lower your risk and make more than your losses just to be break even. But if you keep trading bad, you will need to lower your risk. On the other hand if you trade well, you have the power of compounding on your side. Also simplifies calculation, although if you use MT4, a simple script can take care of that for you. As for the correlation, 3 for any given currency at the same time still seems too much for me, unless you actually divide your risk among them. If you get a decent momentum on a major, it's unlikely that the others would move differently. Think of a Brexit news nowdays, and having positions on GBPUSD, GBPAUD and GBPJPY. But that's just my opinion on this. Psychological Stuff: Thank you received: EDUCATION FORUM MORE LOGOUT I have learned some important psychological things (out of so many others) 571 HOME throughout my journey in RTM this year. For some, this is the same common sense reiterated stuff, but I will put this here anyway, mostly for my personal reminder: 1. Be in a really, really positive mindset when trying to learn new materials. A happy mind would process new information a lot faster than a grumpy, stressed mind. Try it to believe it. A trick that I personally use is to play a favorite song in low volume in the background for a bit before starting to study. 2. Compare yourself not with others, but with your past self. The you today should be better than the you yesterday. The you tomorrow should be better than the you today. Comparing yourself with other people is like comparing apples to kiwis or mangoes. Even if they are in the fruits category, they will never ever be the same. 3. Give yourself time to process new information and believe that you are improving with time, both on and off the charts. The on/off ratio is different for everyone, I guess. For me personally, it is more like 40% on / 60% off ratio. I didn't believe this at first, but gave it a try and some concepts that I didn't understand before seems a lot easier to understand the second time around. 4. Trading is a discipline that needs to be enforced by ourselves. It's a lot different than working in a corporate environment where our boundaries, rules and job description are given to us right from the start. I believe it is why not many people succeed in trading. They fail to set rules and boundaries. 5. Walkthrough of my understanding of the price movement process: 1. Break of blue FL down, immediate target is FL B. EDIT: the next immediate target is next LFL not shown here, but there is a high chance of price filling down to FL B (just to avoid confusion ) 2. At B, ITs would make a decision to break B or to bounce from B. Decision is needed because price has reached its target. We would need to look for PA again at Z. 3. In either case, price would be attracted to (but not guaranteed) the true FL at D, specifically its MPL, because B and C are weaker levels due to the fact they failed to return to pick up the orders. 4. When price reaches FL B and C, we would look for PA also for possible bounces. Bounces would probably be smaller amount due to the fact that they are midrange relative to the move from D to A. H The following user(s) said Thank You: Frosty THANK YOU QUICK REPLY REPLY QUOTE Frosty's Igloo 10 Jan 2018 19:08 #295 HOME EDUCATION FORUM MORE LOGOUT Astrokillah Hi all, An FL is created when an important barrier is broken. This so-called important barrier can be any of the following: opposing FL, important SR, PCP, end of CP, and QM level. Offline When it comes to classifying the scale of an FL, we really only need to use two criteria: Associate Trader 1) It's accomplishment - what time scale does the broken barrier belong to? I don't quit 2) It's visibility Posts: 488 - On what time scale is the FL visible? Thank you received: Now, I see many people still get confused on the issue of scale. When we 762 call something an H4 FL, what do we mean by that, what is an H4 FL? I have not yet come across a definitely guideline on this. Some people call it an H4 FL because it is visible on H4 time frame, but I have recently ask Master IF about this, and he seemed to imply that it's not about visibility, it's more about its accomplishment: So let's say we have a clear swing high/low on H4 being broken/engulfed by a long extended candle (a pole) on H4 timeframe, so we are not going to see a kink on that long candle, but we will find a kink on 1-min chart that coincide with the swing high/low, so this kink visible only on 1-min chart, is actually a H4 kink. And if you can recall, IF has said it several times that we absolutely must go down to the lowest possible timeframe to find our kink. However, a subsequent post by Master IF confused me readthemarket.com/index.php/en/forum/jou...s/1523-if?start=3302 Because he seemed to imply in the above post that visibility is what we should use to classify the scale of an FL. Hence, the quest for the ultimate truth is still on-going.... cheers AK The following user(s) said Thank You: Frosty THANK YOU QUICK REPLY REPLY QUOTE Frosty's Igloo 11 Jan 2018 03:33 #296 Frosty @ bro Rinehart, Thank you for taking your time in explaining to me I believe LQG material is from bro FXS******? It is easily copied and will take a short amount of time to be understood and processed to be re-sold elsewhere, but I've found that inherently lazy people won't even read words that looks too lengthy and heavy. They just want things explained and served to them and you can see their intentions from how they ask questions. I think by placing important posts in a single thread to be put together will not put RTM under more threat as it is already in now (the lurkers and copiers threat). It is already been proven to be unsafe from robbers/thieves as is anyways. The second argument: ease of reference for study. - I've seen all around the forum that many crucial information has been repeated all the time. When these FTRs are engulfed, price then will try to reach the target, which, in my current opinion & Posts: 359 understanding, the next origin of a move on the opposite side. For Thank you received: example, an FTR where price bounced down (short) from a few times, when engulfed, will create an FTR and now this FTR will become the new 571 place where the ITs will load up on longs until the most extreme sets of orders in the next origin of move on the previous down leg has been touched. The logic applies in reverse for shorts. HOME EDUCATION FORUM MORE LOGOUT From the 3 charts from FTR homework #9, I learned that the target for long orders are short orders, and vice versa. After much thinking then the extreme sets of clean orders in the origin DBR/RBD of the previous move up or down must have been the target because this is where the ITs started either buying or selling, i.e. shows their decision. This extreme sets of orders, I believe, is the MPL, which can be seen as a QM level that has not been touched yet. Bro Staurum actually pointed this out on the CHF/JPY chart on post #29 marked with "g". When there is no more orders left there, a new set of orders is coming in. I know some of you can draw this up in seconds By far, this is the hardest RTM material, personally, for me to grasp. Offline When I was finishing the HW, I tried my best not to look at the right side and try to mark things off after moving bar-by-bar. I do this to avoid positive Associate Trader outcome bias (if this is the right term for it), i.e. not looking for the results first and then finding the reason why. What I did was the reverse, i.e. mark Posts: 359 the zones first and then look at the reactions later. Thank you received: When drawing up FLs, I remember what bro Staurum said: 571 without FTR, there is no FL without break there is no FTR without a zone or price barrier, there is no break without price movement, there is no zone or price barrier What I understand the progression of thought is: price movement - barriers/zones - break of barriers - FTR - FL The logical step from that, then, would be: find important barriers, look for their breaks, draw FTRs, assess which are the FLs I will document the steps and thought process that I use in this HW. If it is not too much to ask, I would love to hear any opinions from all of you regarding my thought process when looking for FLs. I might be doing the wrong things and, therefore, over/undercomplicating this. A simple yes/no will suffice, if you all have the time and will to comment, that is Monthly Chart: Find the clearest important barrier starts from this TF for best / largest amounts of pips reaction. This one clearly has an important SR flip level at a BRN at 1.37000 (purple line). I then mark this off in the Monthly chart. I start analyzing at the region of the big bearish candle that breaks the important SR flip from above (shown on the chart) Weekly Chart: I drew the red thick FL from the break of the important SR flip. This also HOME EDUCATION FORUM MORE LOGOUT coincides with the break of a local low (blue line). I trace the chart to the right and found a trick level below (blue box). That was all that I marked the first time. Daily Chart: After the above two charts are done, I went down to the D1 TF. Here, I marked FTB, 2TB, and 3TB. H4 Chart #1: In the H4 TF, I mark the FTR after the break of an important barrier in the LTF (two highs that lines up). Looking back on the D1 chart, I can clearly see now that the level is a FL since it held price up a couple of times before the LQ described on the previous D1 chart. H4 Chart #2: I mark FTB and 2TB to the upper green FL. I can clearly see the LQ base (Pre-CP) and I marked this on the chart. I also marked the swap FL (purple box) from engulfing the top green FL. I can see FTB and 2TB to the purple FL but I didn't mark these on the chart. The LS kink (bottom green box) is then drawn after looking at the H1 chart, which also coincides with the engulfing of a CPPAZ on H1. I then mark the engulf that hasn't reached its target (the pre-CP MPL). Then lastly, the potential BSZ because of the 2 similar lows. HOME EDUCATION FORUM MORE LOGOUT This 2 lows touches the mouth of the LS kink and the previous moves haven't touched the LS kink level. I guessed that this would be used for future fakeout to the MPL of the LS kink. H4 Chart #3: I marked the reaction further on the right side of the LS kink. PA clearly shows the DM forming to touch the exact LS kink level (thin blue box). Personally, the SL for any trade I will take should be placed beyond the trick zone (thin blue box) The target for the long is the pre-CP MPL, which clearly is hit later on. Corrections and comments are more than welcomed Offline Associate Trader HOME EDUCATION FORUM MORE LOGOUT Posts: 359 Thank you received: 571 This week and last week I've been repeatedly watching and analyzing Lite video 1 and 2 to learn something new. Paused trading for a while to get more education since lots of hints were and are being thrown around RTM This time around, what sticks to my brain was something along the lines of HOME EDUCATION FORUM MORE LOGOUT "clean orders that are not picked up on the way away from a zone are very magnetic for price". Time to work this brain harder. The way I understand that statement is that these orders are left for targets for the future retrace after HTF FL engulf, which is when the LTF price wiggles have been completed and all LTF targets have been met and the ITs want to take price into a new HTF playing field (PAZ). So how I understand price movement is that inside a given flag, if all LTF orders are taken (except a clean one on one side) and their LTF targets have been met, ITs will engulf the HTF FL (that has all its orders taken by the LTF wiggle) and price will retrace to collect the clean order that was left on the other side before the engulf happened (provided engulf didn't reach its target yet). A retrace does three things, imho: 1. Shake out weak correct position holders - because ITs are greedy and they want to be the only ones in the correct direction 2. Create more LQ for the ITs (read: retail stop losses) by creating tricks that generate opposite orders to further increase their profits - Greed again 3. Take the very best set of clean order that they left behind to take it to the next target after the engulf - Greed again By reading bro Salivan's Engulf/Retrace article, I also learned that after an engulf, if the next target also has a clean order, the possibility of existing clean orders in the FL price came from before the engulf is very high, which, to my understanding, is a candidate for a higher scale trade since it will fill a greater range/symmetry. Also I concluded FOF = the best set of clean order = clean MPL = untested MPL. Again, need instant slap on the head if this isn't right I'll try to put my ramblings into chart form next, hopefully soon. Day job is always hectic in the 2nd half of the year for the industry I work in. Thank you for reading this long post. Associate Trader Imho, this homework naturally overlaps with caps on price so I guess it is safe to say that I am understanding two things at once from this homework. Posts: 359 Not all of SD zones are marked as the chart would be incredibly messy. I Thank you received: mostly marked caps on price (which is itself a SD zone) that looks significant. By that I mean caps that are created after the break of other 571 caps and also caps that held price down or up at least once. My current understanding on SD zones and Caps on Price: - Price swings from balance to imbalance and back to balance of buyers and sellers repeatedly. - The rallies and the drops are where the imbalance occurs. A rally is an imbalance on the buy side and a drop is an imbalance on the sell side. HOME EDUCATION FORUM MORE LOGOUT - The base is where the balance of transactions between buyers and sellers occur. - The base looks like a square or box. Price finds equilibrium inside the base. The amount of sell vs. buy orders are mostly evenly matched. - The zone between the bottom limit and the top limit of the base is what we call a flag. - SD Zones are decisions whether price is too cheap or too expensive. - Not all SD zones are created equal, i.e. most are reactions to important DBR/RBD we call Flag Limits. - RBR and DBD is, in essence, can be reduced to LTF RBDs or DBRs. In my opinion, it is safe to assume that we will not need to concern ourselves with these two things. - Inside a given HTF SD zone, there are many smaller SD zones that can be drawn. This is due to the fractal nature of the markets. Examples inside A and B on the chart. - When important SD zones (i.e. flag limits) break, we look for the opposite SD zone to be created after their break. This would be the decision that price was not going back inside the base and it is the relevant important one for the next approach. - Ignored SD zones most of the time would be important when price is coming back from the other side. - From my studies, I believe the important caps on price held price up or down at least once. The break of such caps on price will create an important caps on price on the reverse side, which we used to call FTR. - Caps on price are safe to trade if we get CP on approach back to the cap. It is even better to wait for PA/engulf first though for added layer of safety. - Most SD zones are extensively used to create tricks that would provide liquidity for the ITs and, therefore, unreliable as a basis for trades by itself. S/D Article: Prologue/why this article is here: Although the article briefly discusses Supply & Demand, what we are most interested in, is when it breaks. What you should learn by practicing: After finishing your homework you should now be able to answer the question "what are the supply/demand levels themselves reactions to?" My answer: SD levels are reactions to flag limits and it is these flag limits that we should focus our attentions to. If this is already covers everything I need to know, I'll move on next to the next concept, which is SR. I will build upon the chart above. If my knowledge of the caps on price and SD zones are incomplete/flawed, I appreciate any corrections. Salivan Posts: 1489 Thank you received: 6867 THANK YOU QUICK REPLY REPLY QUOTE Homework #1 - CAD/JPY SR Levels 12 Oct 2017 19:22 #191 Frosty @ Master Salivan and Corsica, Thank you for visiting my journal and EDUCATION FORUM MORE LOGOUT HOME checking my understanding I really appreciate it! Next up on the list is Support Resistance Levels (SR) Offline From the markepedia roadmap: Associate Trader S/R Article: P: A S/R level holding so many times in the past must be strong, Posts: 359 right? Well.. think again. Thank you received: W: Again our interest here is to train the eye for where these "infamous" S/R levels break. Later on, in the BSZ article you will see 571 how to take advantage of these stop-loss stacked places. Now I'll post what I have learned before coming to RTM first. Start with the highest TF we can find. For our case, 99.9% of the time it is the Monthly TF (unless somebody has yearly charts ) I marked the closest important MN SR levels relevant to the D1 chart that I was analyzing from the SD homework. The criteria for "important" is: the level should hold price up or down many times, i.e. the SR level should be used a lot of time from both sides. Then I dropped one TF lower on W1 TF. Repeat the same thing again with a different color (red) Again, dropped one TF lower on D1 TF. Repeat the same thing again with another unused color (violet) HOME EDUCATION FORUM MORE LOGOUT I used to think SR levels were all that I need to start trading properly. Mostly the strategy was the usual rejection-type candlestick forming on SR levels. Surely this leads to eventual frustration due to inconsistencies in the setup. And the thing I hated most of all, which I remembered clearly, was that the levels need to be estimated, i.e. not exact to the pip rejection would occur every time. Some days, the rejection candle would print above a support line and some days it would print the candle below the support line. I'm sure you all have tried this type of trading as well at some point before After coming to RTM and reading many materials in this community, I understood (and saw on the charts to prove it myself) that SR is fake propaganda. And the objective of that propaganda is to create liquidity for the ITs. Let me copy paste this definition of "liquidity" from investopedia: Liquidity describes the degree to which an asset or security can be quickly bought or sold in the market without affecting the asset's price. Market liquidity refers to the extent to which a market, such as a country's stock market or a city's real estate market, allows assets to be bought and sold at stable prices. Cash is the most liquid asset, while real estate, fine art and collectibles are all relatively illiquid. Now when I think about the statement "create liquidity", that, in simple terms, would literally mean "create the opposite orders to trade into as not to move price too much" To create liquidity for a truckload of "sell" positions, we would need a truckload of "buy" positions. The reverse applies. Also, by now, it is a public secret that the ITs want to be the only sellers or buyers at every particular time and market. To achieve this maximum greed type of trade, they would need to create price movements that builds up a lot of "stop orders" on price levels they would love to maximize their profits on their positions from. HOME EDUCATION FORUM MORE LOGOUT We know that price changes simply when there are imbalance of orders on the bid or offer side (which was summed up previously on the SD zone and caps HW) Why do the ITs need lots of stops to trade into? Simple: it is because they don't want price to move too much away from their best entry point (hence the need for liquidity at their price levels of interest). If the price level they are going to trade in has only a small pool of stop orders, when they place their large orders, even chunks of it, would immediately cause an imbalance and price would move too much away and eat their potential profits. In order to achieve their goals, i.e. massive amounts of stop orders at desirable price levels, the easiest trick they could do is line up two or three highs/lows. Retailers will see this as an opportunity to fade the SR level once again and place their stops beyond them, where ITs would be the happiest to take these stops. BSZ trade is one of the setups in RTM to exploit this phenomenon. So with the above explanation (and the hint from the markepedia roadmap - Stop loss stacked places), the types of SR manipulations like in these two charts is what I think we should focus on regarding the topic of SR: FXyogi's chart is at the top of my list for this demonstration of how to trade QM using SR levels to FO HOME EDUCATION FORUM MORE LOGOUT My current understanding of SR Levels: - SR levels, by itself, are also unreliable to base our trades upon. Just like SD zones. - Retail SR levels, through my observation from the 3 topmost charts, usually forms at round numbers (decimals ending with 00s and 50s). From previous studies I learned that retailers are basically taught to place stops at the next 20s for shorts and at the next 80s for longs. In my opinion that is why we usually see some form of reaction at the vicinity of BRNs (big, round numbers). This shouldn't be a basis for trade confluence though (from what I concluded from studying charts). - SR levels, especially clean looking ones with identical highs or lows, are mostly traps for retailers to provide liquidity to the ITs. - The only reliable SR levels that I can think of is the SR levels at the highs of UFL and lows of LFL (including the FL from PCP and MPL of CP) along with LS levels, where we would like to look for their kinks/FLs at the rightmost edge of the chart. Of course we are looking at other confluence of factors as well rather than the SR levels themselves. Kink and LS takes the most precedence. - The higher the TF, the more the stops are going to be above/below the fake SR level. - Personally, I would use SR only to find the stops beyond like the bottom two examples. Please excuse the lengthy post. I gave the topic of SR every single thought I can think of. Hopefully I got this topic cleared already. If anything is missing, i appreciate any guidance. From the markepedia roadmap: Compression: P: Often a sign of reversal, compression is used by ITs to pave the way while hiding their true intentions. W: Combined with a valid entry place, it can be a powerful "indicator" for our trade result. You will also see how it is often used as a trap for S/D traders, especially in CPLQ form. This is the SD zones from CAD/JPY previously with the CPs marked. The light blue boxes are the PCP and the MPL of the CP (not all are marked though as my kid wants to dine out like...ASAP!) My current understanding of compression: - Compression (CP) does two things for the ITs: 1. Swapping out their orders from net long to net short or vice versa for their last trade position at the true supply or demand respectively, which, from what I understand, is the MPL. I believe Master Salivan calls this Free Order Flow (Please correct me if I am wrong about your concept of FOF, brother Salivan). How the ITs achieve this swapping of orders is by creating a series of HHs and HLs, in the case of CP to supply, or a series of LHs and LLs in the case of CP to demand. Retailers love to use these definitions of a "trend" as a base for their "trading systems". HHs and HLs would create buy stops above the last highs. What the ITs do is they will push price above the previous high and consume all the buy stops sitting there with their sell positions, which would drop price again due to the absence of bids. ITs are essentially reducing their exposure to their long positions. Retailers would HOME EDUCATION FORUM MORE LOGOUT believe that this drop is a "retrace" and place more buy stops above the new high and the cycle repeats until the ITs are satisfied with their now net short position. The same logic applies for downwards-sloping CP to true demand. 2. Clearing out any pockets of supply or demand in the way of their desired net position. As an example, if ITs desire a net short position from the true supply as in the case of upwards-sloping CP, it would make their lives much more easier if they clear out the pockets of demand during the CP process for ease of movement through the CP zone. What I understand by "clearing out pockets of demand" is to take every last sets of orders in the demand zone, which is also the MPL of the demand zone, on the way up. In a perfectly ideal world, every last sets of opposing orders inside a CP (like MPLs of the demand zones in the previous example) would already be cleared on the way to the true supply or demand. Below is IF's diagram of a perfect CP which I think we all should always keep in mind. - At the end of CP, we would often see an LQ spike. I personally see this as an odd-enhancer as it is effectively used to create euphoria (in the case of breakout traders) and misery (in the case of the last SD or SR fader). As a side note for myself, I personally prefer to use the term "Liquidity Gap" instead of LQ since I believe there would be a shortage of either bids or offers in an LQ, but that is just me being foolishly over-technical . This final LQ push to the true zone would create massive opposite orders for the ITs to add to their positions, e.g. lots more of buy orders for ITs to short into in the case of CP to true supply. - I have also found that some bases of LQ, if aligned with LS levels, could be a nice place to look for bounces, like the below NOK/SEK example. This is already mentioned by IF in the Lite Video #2, which is the Pre-CP (PCP). The other place to look for bounces in price is the MPL of the CP. The area between the MPL of CP and PCP is a flag and we wouldn't want to trade inside there, unless we are trading a lower TF. HOME EDUCATION FORUM MORE LOGOUT - CP in the direction of the ITs desired final net position can also be used for providing a push to the true zone, as we can best see it from IF's example below. - Another thing we should look out for is a HTF level that runs through CPs. IF said (I think it was from the homework analysis section in Lite Video #1) that when we have a HTF SR running through the middle CP, we should divide the CP into two distinct CPs on each sides of the HTF kink, i.e. one CP above with its MPL and PCP, and the other CP below with its MPL and PCP. The way I understood this is that the HTF kink from the HTF SR level has the potential to turn price in the middle of the CP. - Last thing that I can include regarding the concept of CP is that we can look for CP to form after price has hit our zone of interest. The Price Reading Article gave out a clue for this: Reaction In LTF, does price react violently to the first decision point? Does it quickly engulf the nearest S/D? (good sign). Does price simply CP away? Maybe it wants to go to the next decision point. If the reaction from our zone of interest is a CP instead of a liquidity spike in our intended direction of trade, the trade could potentially be in trouble. Again, please excuse the lengthy post. HOME EDUCATION FORUM MORE LOGOUT Continuing with the D1 CAD/JPY chart from the previous homework with the important SD zones and caps on price marked, I have marked three chart sections that demonstrate BSZ scenario. I removed the CP markings for this homework since I don't want to extremely clutter the chart. HTF Chart: CAD/JPY D1 Chart LTF Chart A: LTF Chart B: LTF Chart C: HOME EDUCATION FORUM MORE LOGOUT My current understanding of BSZ: - BSZs are traps that provide liquidity for the ITs in the form of opposite stop orders from the retailers. - BSZs are formed by multiple highs (or lows) that exactly or almost line up together right below the true supply (or right above true demand). From my observations, it is not necessary to have exact highs or lows that line up at the perfectly same price, but they would need to be close together. - The key important point about BSZs is that we should understand where the retailers' stops are clustered by understanding retail SR and trend trading methods, and, therefore, where is the best price level beyond them to trigger all of their stops for the benefit of the ITs. - Another thing from Bro Edge that deserves a mention is that the BSZ is best formed outside the true supply or demand zone. I believe the reason for this is because of two things. One reason is for RR considerations, i.e. more space between the trap zones' highs/lows and the true supply/demand would equal to more profit potential. The second reason is the more important one, which is that we can't be sure if what looks like a BSZ might already hit the best set of orders in the true supply/demand. By having the BSZ forming outside the kink, we can be absolutely sure that the MPL hasn't been hit yet. I believe this is what bro Edge shared with us in this post - To trade a purely BSZ setup, we would only trade from the true supply/demand, i.e. MPL of the Flag Limit, down/up to the Flag Limit that were created after the break of the BSZ barrier and expect no more than that. That is what I have on BSZ. It looks relatively straightforward and there seems to be no ambiguity in the concept as well, so I think this is one of the easier puzzle pieces to understand. I believe I got the main idea down from the above points, but I'm very open to additional guidance and corrections Thank you all! Posts: 359 From the markepedia: Thank you received: FTRs: 571 P: The charts are full of FTRs, learn what it is and how to identify it. W: Flag limits and FTRs are the same, but not every FTR is a Flag Limit. Practice and learn how to use them in your trading. From previous homeworks, bro Staurum actually gave out a nice hint on HOME EDUCATION FORUM MORE LOGOUT this already: without FTR, there is no FL without break there is no FTR without a zone or price barrier, there is no break without price movement, there is no zone or price barrier So I started with SRs that seems obvious up to the monthly level and worked my FTRs from those. I marked all FTRs from the weekly timeframe and moved on to the daily timeframe. The most important part of this exercise is to find the important barrier and the resulting FTR that would be created after. Below is what I have marked for this exercise. Please note that not all FTRs are marked to prevent a mess of colors. I just marked the important ones that are created after a break of significant SR or after a break of previous FTR. My current understanding of FTRs: HOME EDUCATION FORUM MORE LOGOUT - FTRs can be seen all over the chart. To eliminate the worst ones, immediately remove the ones that broke local SRs, i.e. the ones created after the break of a previous highs or lows in the same swing. - FTRs can be created by a break of any barrier, but the most important ones are the FTR after the break of FTRs that held price up or down previously, which we all know as Flag Limits. - FTRs are created by the ITs to either: trap retailers by creating false SD zones or mark their and their IT friends' PAZs. - For the fake SD FTR, from IF's recent diagram explaining FTRs and FLs, these can be seen in FTRs that did not retrace to pick up the orders in the previous kink/FL. ITs create these FTRs to create liquidity from the SD traders, i.e. their stop losses for ITs to trade in the true SD. BSZ concept also comes from this fake SD concept of FTRs - For the true PAZ marker / PAZ delimiter type of FTR, I think there was a different concept in the beginning years of RTM, which causes a lot of confusion in the past. The new concept is that the more hidden the delimiter FTR, the better. as the retailers can't see them. This is what I understand currently: HOME EDUCATION FORUM MORE LOGOUT - The way we use FTRs now is to use them as fake SD zones to expect FO from. The most common way of tricking the retailers are where price reacted off the mouth of an important FTR (the FL), creating a fake SD zone (the FTR of the FL) and then on the next visit fakes to the MPL of the important FTR. Bro H recently wrote a lovely piece on this here . - Remember that all FLs are FTRs, but not all FTRs are FLs. Again, the distinction should be clear from IF's diagram above. - After reading many of bro Salivan's journal, I also believe that FTRs that held price up or down many times are the important ones to look out for. These would be saved later for the FL homework. Orderflow-wise, as in everyday buying and selling in any market, I think it makes the most sense for ITs to open their positions in roughly the same price level or only a few pips off rather than scattering their orders for a certain direction in a wider zone. Thank you all for any corrections and comments. HOME EDUCATION FORUM MORE LOGOUT HOME EDUCATION FORUM MORE LOGOUT My current understanding of FLs: - The best FLs are ones that clearly holds price up or down as these show the price level that ITs use to accumulate their positions, either long or short. - ITs can't create tangible goods or services like normal people and businesses. They create their "goods", i.e. liquidity, by creating tricks. These tricks, if I understood correctly, are basically fakeouts of expected retail behaviors (mostly breakout traders) within the confines of FLs. Master Mertch gave a clear explanation about this concept for us in his journal: When I see peace of PA inside a flag, then for me it became LIMIT.. Thank you Master Mertch - The MPL of FLs are where ITs want to be the only buyer or seller in the market. Movements above or below the MPL, for demand or supply FLs respectively, are only tricks to build up liquidity for the ITs' massive buy or sell orders respectively. - With the above logic, it makes the most sense for us to enter only at MPLs for the highest probability trades. Remember that nothing works 100% of the time. Even trades at MPL could incur losses, possibly due to the taking of clean orders down in the tick charts on the way away from the MPL the first time. Money management should always be kept in check. Unrealistic expectations are the bane of traders. - Also from the above logic, stops for our trades should always be beyond the MPL of the FL we are trading from. - If an FL is engulfed and hasn't reached its target, then the ITs would retrace to pick up orders from the FL that price is coming from. This is how we should trade as taught in RTM: enter on the retrace. - What I learned from Master Salivan (if I didn't misinterpret what he is saying): If the target for an engulf is the origin of the previous move and isn't hit yet, then a deep retrace is required to collect the orders from the MPL (FOF) of the FL we are coming from. - Another thing that I learned from Master Salivan (again, if I didn't misinterpret): An engulf of LTF ORFL, preferably 1-Minute ORFL, is a sign of an engulf, even if the HTF FL is not engulfed yet. Personally for me currently, I think it would be best for beginners like me to keep this concept on the sidelines at the moment and go with the original engulf definition first before moving to advanced Master class concepts. Apologies for the lengthy post. Hopefully I got the concept behind FLs clearly understood there Have a great weekend everyone! The following user(s) said Thank You: staurum, Frosty THANK YOU QUICK REPLY REPLY QUOTE Frosty's Igloo 19 Feb 2017 10:31 #5 Frosty What I have found and learned about CPs are as follows: - Upward-sloping CP to a supply zone = price more likely to go down from the supply zone (provided the last demand in CP PAZ is engulfed down) Offline - Downward-sloping CP from a supply zone = price more likely to go up through the supply zone (provided there is an engulf up of the last supply in Associate Trader CP PAZ and the last demand after CP holds price up) Posts: 359 - Downward-sloping CP to a demand zone = price more likely to go up from Thank you received: the demand zone (provided the last supply in CP PAZ is engulfed up) 571 - Upward-sloping CP from a demand zone = price more likely to go down through the demand zone (provided there is an engulf down of the last demand in CP PAZ and the last supply after CP holds price down) - CP followed by LQ without engulf of the approach flag = potential bounce HOME EDUCATION FORUM MORE LOGOUT later on from base of LQ - CP that didn't activate immediately, i.e. price didn't engulf the last approach flag in the CP, will still be effective at a later date - The best way to trade a CP is to let price engulf the last approach flag, i.e. the last demand or supply in the CP PAZ, and enter on a RT to a previous DP - If price engulfs the last approach flag in the CP PAZ, then price will slide through to the base of CP PAZ - CP can be used by ITs to push price in the direction of a DP if there is not enough order to reach the DP (easier to explain with a chart here ) - CP away from a demand or a supply level can be used later on to create LQ spikes that could trap retail traders into selling or buying into a demand or supply level, respectively, for the ITs to fill in opposite orders (see here for example) Please correct any incorrect interpretations of CP Thank you all for your comments and criticism P.S. Posts: 359 Specifically, I really wondered why IF mentioned LS and LS Kinks to be Thank you received: extremely important. I want to see behind the definition and thought about the things I have been taught previously in other sites and books. What I 571 have thought about and come up with is summed up below. I might look stupid in the process if this isn't correct, but I will definitely learn from you all and what you have to say. Please do correct this reasoning if anything or everything is all wrong as I don't want to dwell on false ideas and beliefs This explanation is for inverted LS (L-H-LL-HH-HL). For a LS (H-L-HH-LL- LH), same thing but in reverse. Below a LS, there are trapped sellers, maybe primarily from breakout strategies (I've traded one myself called the Ross Hook). When price rises above H to make HH, these trapped sellers want to get out with as minimal loss as possible, so they sell as close to the initial L (which is the LS) to minimize losses. This closing of sell orders from the previous break of low of LS comes in near the LS for an obvious reason, i.e. to get as close to breakeven as possible, but since every trapped sellers are doing the same thing, there are naturally some buy orders that are left unfilled. This is because to close a sell order, we need buy orders and the buy orders, in this case, is injected to the market at almost the same price are, which is the LS price level or somewhere close to it. There simply aren't many sell orders around that price area to fill all those incoming buy orders. This created the LS kink, which can be seen as a demand zone in the LTFs. These unfilled buy orders, assuming the ITs' target, i.e. the next important FTR, hasn't been reached, we can safely assume that the ITs will pick up these unfilled buy orders and, afterwards, head for their target. This will be the basis of my 6th FTR homework here. I put this example forward since I think this example shows what I'm trying to explain in the above paragraphs. Again, I might look stupid in the process but for the sake of my own understanding, I'll put this out here to be corrected I would really really appreciate any thoughts on this matter. W: By practicing and doing your PAZ homework you will be able to HOME EDUCATION FORUM MORE LOGOUT identify the direction of price with a much higher success rate. Once Posts: 359 Thank you received: you are able to identify the direction you can then focus on the best 571 spot to enter. My current understanding of PAZs: - Essentially, from my understanding, PAZ is simply the space between FLs. - To use this for our trading, we should just remember that when price enters a new PAZ from one side, price would then want to continue to go to the other side of the new PAZ. - To enter a new PAZ, price need to engulf the previous PAZ's FL. In RTM, we are taught that after price engulfs into a new PAZ without hitting the other side of the new PAZ, we can enter on the retrace to the FL that caused the engulf (many call this the DP/Decision Point, which, from what I understand, is also a FL). - Since PAZs are defined as the space between FLs, then naturally there would be PAZ between PCP MPL and base of CP MPL, between two ends of a pole (LQ gap or wide range bars), and between the top and bottom of flags. The best trades should be taken at the edges of these PAZs, i.e. not inside a PAZ. - With the above logic, then we should be able to classify the regions on the chart as either a FL or a PAZ. - PAZs, like price movement, are fractal in nature as well. What looks like a single PAZ in one timeframe can have many FLs and smaller PAZs inside of it. The choice on how we should define our PAZs is ours to make according to which timeframe/scale we want to trade. - This part is if I didn't misunderstood bro Staurum and bro Enkim: Something that I learned from bro Staurum is that we should always look at the higher scale picture for any possible sign of FO that could happen to the lower scale price movement. Here is the post that hinted on this issue. The clue is to look at the HTF picture. Always make sure that the price movement in the HTF has already cleared the MPL of that HTF FL before trying to trade LTF FLs because if the HTF MPL hasn't been hit, price could fake past all of the LTF FLs and go for the HTF MPL, even if there was a valid engulf to the next PAZ edge. Bro Enkim wrote a note ( here ) that I understood only a few days ago. Thank you bro Staurum and bro Enkim - If price directly hits the other edge of a PAZ, then all trades are off until there is another engulf into another PAZ or if there is a LTF PA telling us to stay within the PAZ (this case, I believe is what we call flagging). Apologies for the lack of charts, but I think the concept of "spaces between FLs" should be a clear picture if FLs has been properly understood. As always, I appreciate any comments, hints, and corrections on this matter. I will start with the Engulf and Fakeout. From the markepedia: Engulf: P: "There is no Price Action without an Engulf" and it is what defines our trading. It is our leading "indicator" regarding price intentions. W: Forget about candle engulfs/engulfing patterns. What we look for is Flag Limit Engulfs. Through homework and experience you will HOME EDUCATION FORUM MORE LOGOUT learn which FL engulfs are real PA. Once a FL is engulfed price will want to go to the next, but we always have to be mindful of how much it can retrace. My current understanding of Engulfs: - Real engulfs or PAs are defined as the consumption of all orders inside FLs, i.e. Engulfs are not simply breaks of local SR, highs and lows, FTRs which are not FLs, or random supply demand zones. I always try to keep this one simple: If I can't find the FL that I think a price movement engulfs, just find the next clear FLs to see the engulf from and scratch the previous mentioned engulf interpretation. - I always try to remember to draw FLs properly first before assessing engulfs from them. The markepedia gave us a hint on this: Through homework and experience you will learn which FL engulfs are real PA. What looks like a PA/engulf, could be a reaction off the real FL or true supply/demand which is obscured inside a large candle in the tick chart. Please see the following example for a real chart example: - The only useful engulf is the one that hasn't reached its target. We should only look for these types of engulfs. Engulfs that has reached its target is non-tradeable - About this part in the markepedia: Once a FL is engulfed price will want to go to the next, but we always have to be mindful of how much it can retrace, through screen time, I concluded that this has to do with the HTF FL that we want to trade from. The important question to ask about an engulf is: Did the engulf came from the last clean sets of orders, i.e. from the MPL? I would be very careful of trying to trade from a FL if previous price movement didn't clear the HTF FL MPL first beforehand, since a DM of the HTF price action could form after an engulf of a LTF FL if the ITs decide to clear all the orders in the HTF FL. This was my first question in this post and the answer to that question is summed up in the previous sentences. In short: Always be mindful of the HTF picture, particularly their MPLs - A personal recipe of master Salivan's engulf, which can be read from the FOF Article states that the ITs can show their intention by engulfing LTF FL, while at the same time, not engulfing the HTF FL. Personally, for a noob HOME EDUCATION FORUM MORE LOGOUT trader such as myself, I believe it is best to leave this advanced master class concept for when we understand all other RTM puzzle pieces. I have not proven this concept myself, but I believe it is safer to wait for whole FL engulfs because there might be clean orders inside an even lower chart than the 1 minute chart, e.g. 10-second or tick charts, above or below the given 1-minute FL. - An important article from master Salivan is the Engulf/Retrace Article . The conclusion that, I think, we should understand from this article is that: an engulf which didn't reach its' target, would surely have left a set or more clean orders from the FL it came from. It is the retrace to this price level with clean orders (FOF) that we should trade from. - Also from the engulf/retrace article, the depth of retrace after an engulf depends on whether the target is an important FL or not (i.e. FTR). Important FLs, from my studies, would have FOs in the form of QM levels that hasn't been touched. Master Mertch's charts show what these FOs look like. If the target is a FL with QM levels that are still clean, then a deeper retrace should be expected as these are ITs' price targets and they would want to get their last orders from the other side of the market to be closed in the FL with clean orders mentioned previously. - Master Staurum created a helpful, three part post on FLs, Engulfs, and Targets with Master Salivan's answer in his journal that is very beneficial for everyone here. - Also important from Master Harry: If you want to find good bouncy places you have to follow the order flow and see where it failed, where there are people (or robots) trapped. - To sum up the wordy, mind-dumping words above: The useful engulf is the one that hasn't reached its target, which signals us to trade in the direction of the engulf from the source FL of the engulf, i.e. from places where people/robots are trapped, while keeping in mind the HTF picture for possible DM/FO scenario As usual, this is what I have learned and not necessarily the truth. The charts, and not my or anyone's words, would provide the ultimate truth. Offline Associate Trader Posts: 359 Thank you received: 571 My current understanding of Fakeouts, Origins/Targets, Ignored Levels, QM, and DM: - To distinguish between FO and engulf, we should look to the FL they are reacting to. While an engulf consumes the FL they are reacting to, an FO stays within the confines of the FL. Please see this modified diagram from IF for my understanding of FOs: HOME EDUCATION FORUM MORE LOGOUT - A great explanation from Master Mertch's journal can be seen here - FO's purpose is to create liquidity. They serve as targets and origins for ITs. These are the "tricks being played" part of the price action puzzle. - A DM should be expected if FO level doesn't quite touch the MPL of the FL price is reacting to. A DM is essentially, Imho, a FO of a FO level. - Targets and origins naturally has a double role. They both are one of the same thing. A long origin/source's target is a short origin/source and a short origin/source's target is a long origin/source. These are essentially LS kink / QM levels inside FLs. - We should check LTF for each QM levels inside the FL we are interested in if we are entering our positions as they may be spiked on the way away from the FL. Do not trade the second and subsequent touches to a QM level if we do not have engulf away from the particular QM level. 1st time back has the most chance of a bounce. - Quote: "Look for tricks being played on approach into places where tricks were played". My understanding of this quote: "Look for tricks being played on approach (BSZ, CP, CPLQ) into places where tricks were played (QM levels or LS kinks inside FLs). All should be done with proper PA/engulf in the right direction. - As for Ignored levels, after many hours of studies, I would stick to just iQM levels that was inside a FL, like in my homework above. My current understanding is that the FO level should be inside the FL to validate the iQM, if the QM level doesn't get respected on the way back. iQM levels that wasn't created inside a FL, I would personally pass. iQM levels would create the next swap FL. Ignored CPs and SRs are left out at the moment. I want to master a few things first at a time. - For QM and DM understanding, at this point in my education, I understand it all comes down to finding pockets of trapped traders and robots, i.e. clean sets of orders. These can be seen through a LS level that hasn't been touched on the way away and, preferably, create some sort of BSZ or CP on the way to the true supply or demand. If we were to zoom in HOME EDUCATION FORUM MORE LOGOUT on LTF, at the tip of a DM, we would likely see a miniature QM formation. DM and QM is a byproduct of the IT manipulation, imho. If MPL was taken by the QM's HH/LL, then QM level or LS kink will work. If MPL didn't get taken by the QM's HH/LL, then expect a DM to form. - My understanding on the details of DM: here - My understanding on the details of QM: here - Ultimately, in the simplest form, as master IF said in one of his videos, we are trading from where the retail stop losses are From all of these technical summaries, I would combine all of these knowledge together to move on to "reading a session" exercises. HOME EDUCATION FORUM MORE LOGOUT What I have learned: Fear is a basic instinct. It keeps us alive and actions are usually immediate. For example, most people in close proximity to dangerous insects or animals would flee instantly. Anxiety, on the other hand, is self-limiting and keeps us immobile. Anxiety paralyzes the mind and hence, the body. This creates invisible boundaries on our capabilities and clouds our judgement. Most of our decisions that comes from anxiety would not be the best ones because we are thinking from an unresourceful state of mind. Anxiety, I believe, comes from irrational worry from going outside our own personal comfort zones. The solution to anxiety is, in my opinion, acceptance of self and forgiveness of self in situations where mistakes were made. Look at mistakes objectively, then learn from them without emotionally charging the event. Most of the time, when we look back on our past experiences, we are blowing the worry out of proportion and when we actually do what we are supposed to do, nothing as bad or catastrophic as the scenario in our minds occurs. Intentional immediate action is also a solution that works for me. I find that the longer I wait and think to do something that I worry about, more negativity comes into the mind.
